### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 聯準會上周三決議升息一碼至 3.75%-4.00%區間，FOMC 政策聲明顯示，18 名
FOMC 成員中有 12 人預計今年將再升息一次 ， 另有4 人認為存在升息兩次的可
能性 ，加上聯準會主席華許(Kevin Warsh)會後表達控制通膨的偏鷹立場 ， 帶動美
元指數走升至 100 高位。
⚫ 歐元區 9 月 ZEW 經濟景氣指數自前月的 31.4 降至 25.8，低於預估的 39.9。數
據下滑顯示在美伊衝突未獲緩解 、 能源價格持續高位情況下 ， 市場對於歐元區經
濟前景抱持較不樂觀的態度。
⚫ 美國公布初請失業金人數為 19.6 萬人，優於市場預期的 20.8 萬，再度呼應聯準
會主席華許(Kevin Warsh)認為目前勞動力市場仍具韌性的說法， 在美國經濟穩
健及通膨頑固的情況下，預計聯準會將持續升息，有利美元持穩。
⚫ 歐洲央行管委暨葡萄牙央行總裁佩雷拉(Alvaro Santos Pereira)表示，歐元區目前

通膨雖未廣泛擴散 ， 惟考量近期天然氣價格飆升 ， 央行將密切關注未來通膨壓力
擴散情形。最新利率期貨市場顯示，歐洲央行今年底仍有再升息一碼空間。
⚫ 日本政府通過消費稅減稅及家庭現金補貼的政策大綱，預計自明年 4 月起的兩
年內，將食品消費稅率自 8%降至 1%，惟大綱中未明確說明資金來源，再度引
發市場對於日本政府的財政擔憂，短線不利日圓表現。
⚫ 台灣央行 9/17 宣布重貼現率連 10 凍維持在 2%不變，央行總裁楊金龍表示，台
灣的通膨相較其他主要經濟體溫和，採取漸進緊縮貨幣政策有助物價穩定。 此
外，目前通膨率為 2%，明年有望降至 2%以下，後續將觀察通膨發展、主要經
濟體貨幣政策緊縮程度及台灣金融情勢，以決定未來貨幣政策走向。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.650，低點在 31.958，以 31.808 作收，較前一周收盤價
升值 1 角 7 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶 ， 美國CPI 數據公布後加強市場對於聯準會 9 月升息的預期 ，
早盤台幣跳空開低，外資連續三日賣超台股並匯出，加以出口商態度轉為惜售，令
台幣接連貶破 31.700、31.800、31.900 關卡， 週四由於聯準會偏鷹派升息，美元指
數走升至 100.300，早盤台幣跳空開低，台幣最低貶至31.958，不過隨後出口商賣壓
出籠，外資雙向進出，台幣收復部分跌幅，維持在31.900 附近震盪；臨近周末，外
資買超台股並於外匯市場匯入 ， 台幣走升至31.722， 最終在央行調節之下收在31.808。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週台幣區間震盪 。 就國際市場而言 ， 美國最新初領失業救濟金人數較預
期減少一萬多人、續領失業救濟金人數亦下降，顯示美國勞動力市場尚屬穩健，加
以聯準會升息抗通膨，鷹派立場給予美元指數支撐、不利台幣表現；就台灣市場來
看，九月台灣央行並未升息，台美利差擴大預計將帶給台幣壓力，此外，儘管聯準
會升息利空出盡，外資買超台股帶動台幣走升，惟台灣即將迎來中秋連假，預計金
融市場交投謹慎，將令台幣陷入橫盤整理格局，綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間
震盪。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/21/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪走升。人民幣中間價連續走升，接連升破 6.7700、6.7600 整數
關口，升至三年七個月來高點，帶動人民幣偏升，不過由於美元指數震盪走強，加
以市場靜待聯準會利率決議公布 ， 交投謹慎 ， 人民幣升幅有限 ， 大致維持高檔震盪 ，
週四聯準會鷹派升息一碼，惟人民幣中間價連續五日走升至 6.7521，並創下四年高
點，帶動人民幣緩步走升，USD/CNH 升破 6.7000 整數大關，終場境外人民幣收在
6.6944，週線升值 94 點，境內人民幣則收在 6.6984，週線升值 21 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣震盪走升 。 就國際市場來看 ，FED 最新政策聲明和經濟預測顯
示，聯準會為明年繼續收緊貨幣政策敞開了大門，美元指數走升，抑制人民幣升值
的速度；就中國國內市場來看，中間價持續偏升將帶動人民幣走升，加以出口商實
質拋匯需求將支撐人民幣表現； 最後就技術面來看 ，USD/CNH 日線圖中 ， 匯價維持
在五日均線之下交投，均線呈現空頭排列，加以USD/CNH 有效跌破 6.7000 整數關
卡，顯示 USD/CNH 空方勢力較盛，綜合以上，預估本週人民幣震盪走升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6700-6.7200

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週日圓走貶。 週初，美國通膨數據維持高位 ，令聯準會升息預期升溫 ，USD/JPY
自 153.29 低點反彈至 155 附近；週間 ，美國聯準會宣布升息一碼 ， 帶動USD/JPY 上
漲至 156.41；臨近週末，日本央行如市場預期升息一碼，惟央行偏鴿派立場令日圓
續貶，帶動 USD/JPY 一度最高來到 158.05，而隨後在日本政府於外匯市場進行匯率
詢價下，USD/JPY 回吐部分漲幅，終場收在 156.86，週線上漲 349 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、日本政府上周通過消費稅減
稅及家庭現金補貼政策大綱，惟大綱中未明確說明財源，再度引發市場對於日本政
府財政惡化的擔憂，預計將不利日圓走勢。二、日本央行上周五決議將政策利率調
升一碼至 1.25%， 9 名決策委員中有 2 人對升息持反對意見，降低市場對日本央行
進一步升息的預期。另一方面 ， 聯準會上周三亦升息一碼至3.75%-4.00%區間 ，FOMC
政策聲明顯示，18 位官員中有 12 人預計今年將再升息一次，另有 4 人認為存在升
息兩次的可能性，聯準會升息立場明顯較日本央行更為鷹派，預計將支撐USD/JPY
走勢。三、日本財務大臣片山皋月近期再度重申，將於必要時採取外匯干預行動，
以維護外匯市場有序運行。四、就技術面而言，在日線圖中，USD/JPY 短中天期均
線向下發散走勢偏空，惟近期匯價在多次向下測試 154 均拉回，明顯下方有買盤支
撐。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：154.80-159.50 
前週交易區間 
153.29 158.05

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，聯準會升息預期升溫加上歐元區 9 月經濟景氣指
數下滑，令歐元走跌至 1.1525；週間，美國聯準會鷹派升息立場帶動美元走升，歐
元最低來到 1.1460；臨近週末，歐元持續偏弱整理，終場收在 1.1483，週線下跌 107
點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、 歐洲央行管委卡薩克斯
(Martins Kazaks) 表示 ， 為避免美伊戰爭引發的燃料成本上漲開始傳導至工資和其他
商品價格，歐洲央行可能需要漸進式調升利率以抑制通膨。最新利率期貨市場顯示
歐洲央行今年仍有升息一碼的空間，預計將支撐歐元走勢。二、美國聯準會決議將
利率調升一碼至 3.75%-4.00%區間，聯準會主席華許會後偏鷹言論帶動美元指數走
升至 100 上方，預計將限縮歐元走升空間。三、歐元區 9 月 ZEW 經濟景氣指數自
前月的 31.4 降至 25.8，低於預估的 39.9。數據下滑顯示在美伊衝突未獲緩解、能源
價格持續高位情況下，市場對於歐元區經濟前景抱持較不樂觀的態度，將不利歐元
表現。四、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 近期回測年線未能有效突破後明顯
拉回轉為下行走勢，目前匯價承壓於各天期均線下方，空方動能較佔優勢。綜合以
上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。

＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1350-1.1590 
前週交易區間 
1.1450 1.1599

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                           新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.14-115.09.18 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.67% ~ 1.70% 
公債－10 年期-A15106 1.92 % ~ 1.921% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.86% ~ 1.861%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ， 本週央行理監事會議決議結果 ， 維持政策利率不變 ， 符合
市場預期 ， 且近兩週央行持續減發定存單 ， 令市場短錢充裕 ， 惟週五銀行間分
配略為不均，部分銀行有資金需求，因此短率持穩於近期區間。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.70%，利率高位較上週上升 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週市場交投清淡，5 年期指標券 A15108 週三等殖系統成交在 1.86%，10 年
期指標券 A15106 則未成交，最近成交利率為 1.92~1.921%。 
台灣央行理監事會議如市場預期維持政策利率不變 ， 總裁楊金龍表示 ， 今年國
內通膨仍屬可控，明年有望回落至 2%以下，市場對今年升息疑慮有所降溫，
對台債形成支撐 ， 惟國際債市走勢仍偏空 ， 預期台債殖利率短線仍呈高檔區間
盤整格局，後續關注下週三財政部公布的第四季公債發行計劃，預估 5 年券
區間為 1.79%~1.83%，10 年券區間為 1.90%~1.95%。

Document 059: 2026年9月宏观数据点评：9月宏观数据总体改善，三季度经济增长动能回升

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年9月宏观数据点评：9月宏观数据总体改善，三季度经济增长动能回升.pdf
Report source: 东方金诚
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

东方金诚宏观研究

1 
9 月宏观数据总体改善，三季度经济增长动能回升 
——2026 年 9 月宏观数据点评 
首席宏观分析师 王青  研究发展部执行总监 冯琳 
事件：据国家统计局2026 年10 月19 日公布的数据，2026 年三季度GDP 同比增速为4.5%，二季度为
4.3%，前三季度 GDP 增速为 4.6%，去年全年 GDP 增速为 5.0%；9 月规模以上工业增加值同比实际增速为
5.5%，8 月为5.2%，1-9 月为5.3%，去年全年为5.9%；9 月社会消费品零售总额同比增速为1.1%，8 月为
0.4%，1-9 月为 1.1%，去年全年为 3.7%；1-9 月全国固定资产投资累计同比下降 7.2%，前值为-7.2%，去
年全年为-3.8%。 
基本观点： 
总体上看，主要受出口增速大幅加快，消费增速改善及投资降幅收窄带动，三季度经济增速企稳回
升，新旧动能转换持续推进。9 月工业生产加速，主要受采矿业以及电力、燃气及水的生产和供应业增加
值增速回升拉动，当月制造业增加值增速受“中秋错期”扰动小幅放缓，但仍处较快增长水平。前三季
度，受内需走弱、部分行业限产影响，工业增加值增速较上年总体有所回落，但在出口 增速高位上行、
高技术产业增加值两位数增长带动下，三季度工业生产增速较二季度明显加快，是当季经速增速上行的
主要推动力量。消费方面，9 月社零增速加快，主要受去年同期基数走低，“中秋错期”对今年9 月社零
同比表现形成正向拉动，以及电子产品、石油制品等商品涨价影响。受上年同期基数下行等影响，三季
度社零增速较二季度有所上行，但当前消费偏弱局面仍有待改善。投资方面，1-9 月固定资产投资同比增
速为-7.2%，降幅与前值持平，三大投资板块增速为“两降一升”。其中，1-9 月基建投资同比降幅略有
扩大，但在稳增长政策发力支撑下，9 月当月投资降幅收窄；9 月8000 亿新型政策性金融工具开始投放，
1-9 月制造业投资同比降幅较前值收窄；最后，三季度房地产市场延续调整，1-9 月房地产投资同比降幅
扩大至-20.6%，是当前整体投资负增长的最大拖累因素。 
往后看，四季度出口仍然有望保持 20%以上的高增速，9 月末以来系列稳增长政策出台后，四季度投
资增速有望止跌回稳，楼市调整幅度将会收敛，低基数下居民消费增速也将有所回升。这将带动四季度
工业生产增速上行，服务业生产增速也有望加快。初步估计，四季度 GDP 增速将加快至 4.7%左右；全年
GDP 增速将在 4.6%附近，落在“4.5%至 5.0%”的目标范围之内。我们预计，着眼于稳定明年一季度宏观
经济运行，后期包括全面降息在内，总量型稳增长政策还有发力空间。

### Page 2

东方金诚宏观研究

2 
   具体解读如下： 
一、三季度 GDP 增速企稳回升，背后主要是当季出口增速高位上行，外需对经济增长的拉动力增强，
同时，三季度商品消费增速也有所加快，投资降幅略有收窄。这带动当季工业生产显著加快，新旧动能
转换提速。在系列稳增长政策出台后，预计四季度 GDP 增速将进一步回升至 4.7%左右，完成全年经济增
长目标已没有悬念。 
三季度 GDP 增速达到 4.5%，较二季度加快 0.2 个百分点，呈现企稳回升势头。背后的主要推动因素
是三季度出口增速加快。以美元计价，当季出口同比增长28.4%，较二季度加快 8.3 个百分点，并带动当
季贸易顺差同比扩大 27.2%，较二季度上升 26.4 个百分点，意味着三季度净出口对经济增长的拉动力显
著增强。此外，三季度国内投资降幅有所收窄，居民商品消费增速改善，也对经济增长动能转强有一定
支撑作用。供给方面，三季度出口高增叠加 以高技术制造业为代表的新动能领域增长势头强劲， 共同带
动当季工业生产增速达到5.1%，较二季度加快0.4 个百分点，是当季GDP 增速加快的主要推动力量。 
三季度宏观经济的主要亮点在于，在 AI 投资热潮推动下，当季高技术制造业和现代服务业增速高位
上行，新旧动能转换加 快，整体就业市场保持稳定 ，物价水平温和回升。数据显示，三季度代表整体经
济物价水平的 GDP 平减指数同比为 1.5%，连续两个季度保持正增长。这意味着此前的物价低迷局面得以
扭转，有助于宏观经济走出“物价负增长——经济增长动能减弱”的负向循环。 
往后看，四季度出口仍然有望保持 20%以上的高增速，9 月末以来系列稳增长政策出台后，四季度投
资增速有望止跌回稳，楼市调整幅度将会收敛，低基数下居民消费增速也将有所回升。这将带动四季度
工业生产增速上行，服务业生产增速也有望加快。初步估计，四季度 GDP 增速将加快至 4.7%左右；全年
GDP 增速将在 4.6%附近，落在“4.5%至 5.0%”的目标范围之内。我们预计，着眼于稳定明年一季度宏观
经济运行，后期包括全面降息在内，总量型稳增长政策还有发力空间。 
二、9 月工业生产加速，主要受采矿业以及电力、燃气及水的生产和供应业增加值增速回升拉动，当
月制造业增加值增速受“中秋错期”扰动小幅放缓，但仍处较快增长水平。前三季度，受内需走弱、部
分行业限产影响，工业增加值增速较上年总体有所回落，但在出口 增速高位上行、高技术产业增加值两
位数增长带动下，三季度工业生产增速较二季度明显加快。 
9 月规上工业增加值同比增长5.5%，增速较上月加快0.3 个百分点，工业生产总体延续回升态势。分
门类看， 9 月采矿业增加值同比增长 1.3%，增速较上月回升 2.7 个百分点，是当月工业生产增速加快的
主要拉动项。采矿业生产增速回正，主要受两方面因素带动：一是煤矿复产稳步推进，9 月规上原煤产量
同比下降 6.8%，降幅较上月收窄 0.9 个百分点；二是原油生产提速，9 月规上原油产量同比增长 2.2%，

### Page 3

东方金诚宏观研究

3 
增速较上月加快 1.8 个百分点。9 月电力、燃气及水的生产和供应业增加值同比增速也有所加快，从上月
的 4.9%回升至5.6%，主要与去年同期基数走低有关。 
作为工业最大门类，9 月制造业增加值同比增长6.0%，增速较上月小幅回落0.1 个百分点，但仍处较
快增长区间。当月制造业生产较快增长，主要受三方面支撑：一是出口保持高增，9 月规上工业企业出口
交货值同比增长 17.0%，增速较上月加快 5.9 个百分点，对生产的拉动进一步增强。二是高技术制造业延
续两位数增长，当月增加值同比增长 15.3%，前值 16.7%。三是国内稳增长政策发力，带动投资、消费下
行压力边际缓和。需要说明的是，9 月制造业增加值增速小幅回落主要受中秋假期错期扰动——今年中秋
落在 9 月、去年在10 月，导致今年9 月工作日比上年同期少1 天，并不意味着制造业景气走弱。 
累计看，1-9 月规上工业增加值同比增长5.3%，增速较上年全年放缓0.6 个百分点，较上半年小幅放
缓0.1 个百分点。今年以来工业生产增速有所回落，一方面受内需走弱拖累，另一方面“反内卷”持续推
进，煤炭等行业限产，也对工业生产形成一定下拉。不过，与需求端投资、消费相比，今年以来工业生
产仍维持较快增长，宏观经济“供强需弱”特征较为明显。这背后有两大支撑： 
一是出口持续高增，外需对工业生产的拉动作用不断增强。1-9 月规上工业企业出口交货值同比增长
10.7%，增速较上年全年加快 8.5 个百分点，较上半年加快 1.0 个百分点。二是在市场供需两旺、财政金
融政策支持力度持续加大的有利环境下，1-9 月高技术产业增加值同比增长 14.3%，增速较上年全年加快
4.9 个百分点，其中，三季度同比增速为16.3%,二季度为 14.0%。目前高技术制造业在规模以上工业生产
中的占比在17%左右，能够对整体工业生产形成较为强劲的拉动作用。 
值得一提的是，三季度工业生产较二季度有所加快，是推动 GDP 增速上行的主要推动力量。数据显
示，当季工业增加值同比增速为 5.1%，较二季度加快 0.4 个百分点，主要受出口大幅走强以及高技术产
业生产加速拉动。 
展望四季度，出口有望保持高增，国内稳增长政策发力将带动投资改善， 居民消费增速也将有所回
升，叠加高技术制造业生产将延续较快增长，以及去年同期基数走低，预计工业生产增速总体上将较三
季度进一步加快。考虑到“中秋错期”对工业生产的扰动将在 10 月消退，当月工业增加值增速有望加快
至 5.7%左右。 
三、9 月社零增速加快，主要受去年同期基数走低，“中秋错期”对今年9 月社零同比表现形成正向
拉动，以及电子产品、石油制品等商品涨价影响； 受上年同期基数下行等影响，三季度社零增速较二季
度略有上行，但当前消费偏弱 局面仍有待改善。 背后是受补贴政策退坡、房地产市场持续 调整、居民消

4 
费信心偏弱等因素叠加影响 ，同时，二季度以来 城镇居民人均可支配收入中位数同比增速 放缓，也是近
期消费增速仍然偏低的重要原因。 
9 月社零同比增长1.1%，增速较上月加快0.7 个百分点，分项中，商品零售和餐饮收入同比分别增长
0.9%和 1.2%，增速较上月分别加快 1.6 和 0.6 个百分点。当月社零增速回升，背后主要是去年同期基数
走低，以及“中秋错期”对今年9 月社零同比表现形成正向拉动。同时，从环比看，9 月社零季调环比增
长 0.35%，明显好于上月的下降 0.16%，显示消费增长动能边际改善，但仍低于过去十年同期 0.83%的平
均增长水平，消费偏弱局面仍有待逆转。 
从限额以上商品零售额看，汽车销售下滑继续对社零整体增速产生较大拖累。受新能源车购置税免
税政策退坡、前期以旧换新政策透支居民购车需求等因素影响，9 月汽车零售额同比下降 21.2%，降幅较
上月扩大2.7 个百分点。剔除汽车后，9 月除汽车以外的消费品零售额同比增长3.9%，明显高于当月社零
整体增速。 
从其他商品来看，9 月限额以上各类别商品零售额增速普遍加快，主要受基数走低及“中秋错期”影
响。值得一提的是，尽管去年同期基数有所走高，9 月通讯器材零售额同比增速仍较上月加快3.8 个百分
点至 31.1%，石油及制品零售额同比降幅较上月收窄 6.2 个百分点至-0.8%，这主要受相关商品价格上涨
拉动。不过，9 月家电零售额同比增速较上月放缓 0.4 个百分点至 1.9%，金银珠宝、体育娱乐用品、家
具、建筑装潢材料零售额延续负增长，尽管降幅与上月相比有不同程度收窄，这主要受补贴政策拉动效
果减弱、房地产市场持续下行、居民消费信心偏弱等因素叠加影响。 
累计看，1-9 月社零同比增长 1.1%，增速较上年全年放缓 2.6 个百分点，较 1-6 月放缓 0.2 个百分
点。从当季表现看，三季度社零同比增长 0.7%，较二季度 0.2%的增速有所反弹，主要是受上年同期基数
下行影响。同时，1-9 月服务零售额累计同比增长5.0%，增速较上年全年回落0.5 个百分点，较1-6 月回
落 0.3 个百分点。需要指出的是，受“中秋错期”影响，9 月当月服务零售额同比增长约5.8%，增速较8
月大幅加快1.6 个百分点。 
今年以来，尤其是二季度以来，社零表现走弱，服务消费增速亦有所走低，主要与补贴政策退坡、
房地产市场延续下行等因素有关。其中，房地产市场下行是主要拖累，这不仅直接抑制涉房消费，也在
持续影响居民消费信心。数据显示，8 月消费者信心指数录得89.5，仍明显低于100 的中值水平。同时值
得关注的是，今年 1-9 月城镇居民人均可支配收入累计同比增长 4.5%，增速较一季度和上半年分别加快
0.3 和 0.1 个百分点，但人均可支配收入中位数同比增速较一季度和上半年分别放缓0.5 和 0.1 个百分点

东方金诚宏观研究

5 
至 3.9%。收入均值与中位数增速背离，表明整体收入改善主要受人工智能及有色金属等少数行业居民收
入大幅上行带动，其他多数中低收入群体收入改善相对滞后。这也是二季度以来消费增速总体放缓的重
要原因。 
展望后续，接下来财政金融协同促消费政策有望进一步加力，各地财政部门会出台更多促消费“自
选动作”，国内促消费政策力度有望加大，加之上年同期低基数影响，四季度社零同比有望加速。我们
也认为，四季度加发5500 亿地方政府债券，其中3000 亿用于提高县区一般公共预算保障能力，也有助于
推动四季度消费增速加快。其中，10 月“中秋错期”因素拉动作用退去，社零增速有可能会小幅放缓，
但仍有望保持在1.0%左右，而在低基数带动下，11 月和12 月社零同比增速将加速至2.0%附近。 
四、投资：1-9 月固定资产投资同比增速为-7.2%，降幅与前值持平，三大投资板块增速为“两降一
升”。其中，1-9 月基建投资同比降幅略有扩大，但在稳增长政策发力支撑下，9 月当月投资降幅收窄；
9 月 8000 亿新型政策性金融工具开始投放，1-9 月制造业投资同比降幅较前值收窄；最后，三季度房地
产市场延续调整，1-9 月房地产投资同比降幅扩大至-20.6%，是当前整体投资负增长的最大拖累因素。 
分季度来看，三季度固定资产投资同比增速为 -10.4%，降幅略小于二季度的-11.1%。数据显示，三
季度三大投资板块中，除房地产投资降幅扩大外，基建投资、制造业投资降幅均较二季度有所收窄。 
基建投资方面，1-9 月投资增速为-4.2%，降幅较前值扩大 0.2 个百分点；据我们测算，三季度基建
投资同比下降 7.9%，二季度下降 8.2%。其中，9 月当月基建投资同比下降 5.4%，降幅较上月收窄 1.5 个
百分点。我们认为，9 月当月基建投资降幅收窄，背后的主要推动因素包括：9 月国家发改委等部门加力
推动“六张网”等重点项目建设；当月地方政府专项债发行 6200 亿，为年内最高水平，其中用于化债的
资金规模为 1800 亿，占比明显下降，用于项目建设的资金规模为4400 亿，创年内最高——这或表明，9
月化债对项目资金的挤占效应显著减弱，基建投资动能增强；最后，9 月 8000 亿元新型政策性金融工具
启动投放，也对当月基建投资形成较强拉动作用。 
往后看，伴随9 月末央行宣布降低PSL 利率，将“六张网”建设纳入支持范围，加之四季度财政支出
和各类政府债券资金使用赶进度，特别是 8000 亿新型政策性金融工具在四季度全面推进，以及加发的
5500 亿地方政府债券中将有2500 亿用于项目建设，这些都将推动“六张网”和重大项目建设进度显著加
快。我们判断，年底前基建投资增速有望由负转正。这是接下来“加大逆周期调节力度”的主要体现，
也会成为推动今年投资“止跌回稳”的主要拉动因素。 
制造业投资方面，1-9 月累计同比增速为-2.1%，降幅较前值收窄 0.2 个百分点。据我们测算，三季
度制造业投资同比下降 3.8%，二季度为同比下降 3.9%。其中，9 月制造业投资同比下降 0.9%，降幅较 8

### Page 6

东方金诚宏观研究

6 
月显著收窄 5.2 个百分点，主要原因是当月稳投资政策发力，特别是8000 亿新型政策性金融工具开始投
放，其中部分资金用于芯片制造等先进制造业投资。另外，上年同期投资基数大幅下行，也会推动三季
度制造业投资同比数据改善。需要指出的是，受政策倾斜叠加市场供需两旺驱动，当前高技术产业投资
处于较快正增长状态，1-9 月同比增速达到4.9%。其中，航空、航天器及设备制造业，电子及通信设备制
造业投资分别增长12.5%、7.6%。 
往看后，着眼于推动投资止跌回稳，接下来 8000 亿新型政策性金融工具、今年超长期特别国债等支
持的制造业项目投资会加快推进，会对四季度制造业投资有支撑作用。我们判断，1-10 月制造业投资下
行势头会进一步收窄，年底前月度制造业投资同比增速有望回正。 
房地产投资方面，1-9 月累计同比增速为-20.6%，降幅较前值扩大 0.7 个百分点。据我们测算，三季
度房地产投资同比下降26.4%，二季度同比下降23.0%，其中，9 月房地产投资同比下降26.3%，降幅较8
月扩大0.8 个百分点。近期房地产投资下滑幅度扩大，背后是三季度以来房地产市场延续调整。其中，三
季度新房、二手房销售增速都有所下行，70 城二手房房价指数环比跌幅也略有扩大。另外，当前金融机
构对房企融资仍然偏于谨慎。 
9 月 29 日，全国范围内针对刚需购房实施定向贴息的政策出台，预计将对楼市有一定提振作用。不
过，8 月 28 日公布系列房地产新政，主要内容包括推动商品房现售，完善房地产开发贷管理，延长居民
按揭贷期限等，核心是将 “房住不炒”制度化，加快构建房地产行业发展新模式。这有可能在短期内加
大房企资金压力，接下来房地产投资还将较大幅度下行。我们预计，1-10 月房地产投资同比降幅有可能
扩大至21.0%左右。总体上看，年底前房地产投资降幅收窄的难度很大。

元富證券投資顧問股份有限公司 台北市敦化南路二段 97 號 19 樓 (02)2325-3299  (1 15)金管投顧新字第 016 號 
公司拜訪快報

台半(5425) 
建議：維持買進 
結論：2027 年產品漲價效益將更全面

一、台半為功率元件製造與封測廠商，車用營收占比最
高 
台半成立於 1979 年並為功率元件製造與封測廠商，晶
圓製造有宜蘭六吋廠和天津四吋廠，後段封測則有天津
和山東兩座廠房，半導體以及條碼印表機(子公司鼎漢
3611 TT, 持股 35.74%)分別占 1H26 營收比重為 38%和
62%，半導體業務主要產品包含 MOSFET、保護元件
TVS 和 ESD、二極體，MOSFET 產品線主要透過和晶
圓代工廠如聯電合作，非 MOSFET 產品線則以自製為
主。若以產品應用而言，車用、工業、3C 和其他分別占
1H26 營收比重為 59%、16%、23%和 2%。因MOSFET
業務拓展有成，營收占比由 2024 年的中個位數提升至
目前 20%，且車用客戶結構由中系擴展至歐美客戶，產
品組合優化為毛利率改善另一主因。 
二、報價逐季調整，2H26 營收獲利可望季季高 
除 7 月調整部分產品價格外，鼎翰併購 Bluebird 綜效顯
現並開始出貨大型企業專案，預估 3Q25 營收 52.35 億
元(+3%QoQ)，受惠於無一次性晶圓報廢影響以及產品
漲價效益，毛利率將由上季之 29.6%提升至 30.8%，營
業利益 5.57 億元(+18%QoQ)，稅後淨利 2.53 億元
(+25%QoQ)，EPS 1.03 元。展望 4Q26，受惠於更多品
項產品將於 11 月漲價，預期 4Q26 營收 53.53 億元
(+2%QoQ)，預估毛利率 31.7%優於前季之 30.8%，營
業利益 6.26 億元(+13%QoQ)，稅後淨利 3 億元
(+19%QoQ)，EPS 1.22 元  
三、去中化轉單效益，以及AI 強勁需求改善產業供需為
營運成長兩大動能 
去年 10 月安世半導體之亂以及今年 4 月揚杰科技被歐
盟列入制裁名單，皆引發全球功率半導體去中化轉單效
應，此兩家業者與台半產品重疊性高，其中安世半導體
轉單效益推估增加單季營收達 3-4 百萬美元，因車用客
戶驗證時程冗長，後續訂單延續性高，至於揚杰科技轉
單效益，經營階層認為尚不顯著。此外，Onsemi 於法
說會上將 AI 資料中心功率半導體市場規模由原先預估
的 120 億美元大幅調升至 500 億美元，雖 AI 營收僅占
公司營收個位數 ， 但AI 外溢需求致使成熟製程產能排擠，
改善功率元件產業供需平衡與價格，且國際 IDM 大廠
將資源轉移至 AI 用高瓦數電源模組與高階 SiC/GaN，
此亦致使車用與工控 MOSFET、二極體供給較過去吃
緊。 
四、產品漲價效益尚未完全反映 
全球功率半導體去中化轉單效應 ， 且今年以來Infineon、
TI、NX、STM 以及士蘭微等多家大廠陸續調漲報價，皆
反映出功率元件市場供需改善，且銅、金價格居高不下
致使成本增加，加計委外晶圓代工和封測成本皆增加，
故致使台半今年 4、7 和 11 月三次調漲報價，但今年產
品漲價效益僅限於產能占比近半之非合約客戶。投顧認
為台半今年毛利率改善幅度來自於稼動率提升勝於產品
漲價因素 ， 然功率元件產業受惠去中化轉單和AI 強勁需
求趨勢不變，加計反應委外晶圓代工和封測上揚成本，
預期 2027 年台半產品漲價效應將擴及部分合約客戶。  
五、維持買進理由 
1)預估 2026 年和 2027 年 EPS 分別為 3.78 元和 5.89
元。 
2)考量目前產品上漲僅限於非合約 ，2027 年產品漲價效
益更加全面，給予買進評等，TP 130 元 (2027 PER x 
22)。

Document 315: FX Daily_ Inflation vs. AI overbuild story dominates

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ Inflation vs. AI overbuild story dominates.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Financial markets have calmed a little after Coreweave stock
bounced on Thursday. But the debate between intensifying
price pressures needing tighter monetary policy and a tech
correction requiring a softer policy outlook continues. Expect
more consolidation in FX markets today and more interest in
local stories, such as Indonesia's equity travails

Chris Turner, Frantisek Taborsky and Francesco Pesole
FX markets remain caught between intensifying inﬂation and a re-pricing of AI
risk
USD: What does the ﬂatter yield curve
mean?
The sell-oﬀ on Coreweave assets saw a modest reversal yesterday
after Nvidia's CEO, Jensen Huang, said that Coreweave's ﬁnancial
strength was improving and its cost of capital was declining.
Perhaps those comments should not be surprising from a major
shareholder in Coreweave; let's see how long this bounce in
Coreweave assets lasts. The big takeaway this week has been the
recovery of the long-end of the US Treasury market, even as

### Page 2

inﬂation data continues to come in hot. Here it seems that
investors are starting to ﬁnd value in long-dated Treasuries and are
viewing those as a middling hedge against a correction in the AI
trade. Were the tech sell-oﬀ to gain momentum, that Treasury
buying would move to the shorter end of the curve.
While fears of an AI correction may be growing, inﬂation data
continues to come in hot. Yesterday it was US September PPI data
blowing away consensus. And today, import prices should come in

near 8% year-on-year, the highest since summer 2022. Inﬂation
data is keeping short-dated interest rates supported and has
delivered a near 20bp ﬂattening in the 2-10 year Treasury curve
this week.
What does that mean for FX? Typically, an environment where the
Fed is applying the brakes while growth prospects are being revised
down is good for the dollar against activity and emerging market
currencies. It is only when short end rates break lower that the

dollar is ready to drop. And we are not there yet.
Barring another big leg down in AI stocks and risk assets in general,
expect the dollar to consolidate near recent highs. We would
continue to expect some defensive outperformance of the Swiss
franc in this environment. DXY will probably struggle to get above
the 102.40/50 area now, while support is 101.75/80.
Chris Turner
EUR: Inﬂation to be conﬁrmed at 3.8%
today
Today's eurozone data should see September CPI conﬁrmed at

3.8% – well above the European Central Bank's 2% target. Markets
are still reeling from the hawkish set of inﬂation forecasts delivered
at the ECB September press conference, a key driver of the near
75bp of further tightening expected from the ECB. Our team looks
for just one more hike, however, and developments in French bond
markets will have a big say in whether the ECB needs to tighten
much more from there.

### Page 3

EUR/USD has received a lift from this week's unease in US AI stocks
and may be entering a decent consolidative phase now. This could
be worth a move all the way to 1.1340/50. But do not expect
EUR/USD to be the big outperformer on a further US equity
correction. We think a lower USD/CHF would take the lead there.
Chris Turner
CEE: Wage embers and price sparks
The CEE week ends with mostly second-tier data. Hungary releases
August wage ﬁgures this morning, one of the central bank’s

remaining domestic obstacles to achieving stable inﬂation. We
forecast wage growth of 7.1%, still well above CEE peers, while the
agreed minimum wage increase raises the risk of renewed wage
pressures next year.
Later, the Czech Republic publishes September producer prices.
Alongside agricultural prices, these suggest inﬂationary pressures
are broadening beyond fuel costs, posing an upside risk to the CPI
outlook, as discussed in our CEE report earlier this week. Turkey will

also release September inﬂation expectations, the ﬁnal relevant
data point before next week’s central bank meeting. We expect the
Central Bank of Turkey to resume its easing cycle, which was
paused after the US-Iran conﬂict began, by cutting the policy rate
from 37.0% to 36.0%.
CEE ﬁxed income saw a strong rally yesterday, tracking core
markets despite negative global sentiment; Hungary and Romania
have outperformed the rest of the region over the past two days.

We maintain the view that the koruna and zloty yield curves cannot
decouple from the global narrative, and that higher energy prices
will keep rate hikes priced in for longer. The Hungarian curve will
face a test next Monday or Tuesday with the release of the draft
budget for next year – an event drawing signiﬁcant market
attention, though expectations may be overly optimistic. FX, on the
other hand, remains more or less stable, and we continue to hold

our bearish views for the CEE region in general.
Frantisek Taborsky

Read more
IDR: MSCI downgrade warning sinks local
assets
In a statement that will have been a diﬃcult read for Indonesian
authorities, MSCI said overnight that it was launching a
consultation to reclassify MSCI Indonesia indexes from Emerging

Market to Standalone Market status. Merely dropping Indonesia to
Frontier Markets would not properly recognise the depth of the
market problems, says MSCI. These problems are opaque
shareholding structures and suspected coordinated trading
behaviour, according to MSCI.
Local equity indices fell 10-11% on the announcement and an
already weak rupiah gapped above the 18000 threshold. Expect
more FX intervention from Bank Indonesia to protect this year's

high at 18200 – but clearly pressure for a weaker rupiah is building.
Please see ING's Deepali Bhargava's latest comments on USD/IDR
here.
Indonesia's problems serve as a reminder that EMFX will
increasingly be driven by local stories over the coming quarters.
How the AI trade fares will dominate Korea and Taiwan, Brazilian
assets could be re-rated further on a Bolsonaro presidency, and
Hungary's euro ambitions will have a major say on local asset

markets. Please see our latest thinking on this topic in October's
edition of FX Talking.
Chris Turner

Document 321: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

Exhibit 1: Rising yields and uncertainty have increased the cost of capital 
30-year government bond benchmark yields (%) 
          













### Page 2

As equities have continued to outperform bonds, equity risk premia have fallen back to 
levels last seen in the late 1990s, leaving equity markets more vulnerable to further 
increases in bond yields. That said, in the US and Japan, the ERP has bounced off recent 
lows (Exhibit 3).

3. The impact of rising bond yields, however, depends not only on the level of yields, but 
also on the reasons as well as the speed of the adjustment (Exhibit 4). It is the speed 
issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

reflected in other regions too. Today, a 2-standard deviation move in 10-year Treasury 
yields would equate to about 50bps over a month or 30bps over two weeks, similar to 
the adjustments in recent days which explains the pullback in equity prices.

4. But despite the rise in yields, nominal GDP and profit growth remain strong and, until 
recently, the impact of rising bond yields has been offset by the strength in corporate 
profits. Earnings growth has been the main driver of equity returns over the past 18 
months in all regions (Exhibit 5). This strength has meant that PE multiples have either 
been flat (Japan and Europe) or have fallen (the US, Asia and EM).

In the US, in particular, the forward PE multiple for the S&P has come down from 22x at

### Page 4

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being 
close to its all-time high. 
5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

Exhibit 6: US equities have underperformed other major regions since 2025

### Page 5

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

Exhibit 7: Earnings revisions have unusually been positive

### Page 6

The combination of higher cost of capital and greater capital intensity in the tech sector 
has reduced the value of their future cash flows and triggered a de-rating in the biggest 
companies.  The dominant capex hyperscalers in the US now have a forward PE close to 
the average of the rest of the market.

8. Software companies have also de-rated on the back of fears of disruption from AI 
while memory and chips stocks have also experienced lower multiples as their earnings

### Page 7

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

Exhibit 10: Industrials now trade at higher valuations than Technology

Goldman Sachs Global Strategy Views

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

### Page 9

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

Even within sectors like technology, correlations have fallen sharply (Exhibit 13). The 
‘magnificent seven’ that was long seen as a homogeneous group of technology winners 
has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

陣圖顯示2026年仍有再升息一碼空間 ，2027
年傾向維持利率不變 ，2028年後才逐步回到
降息趨勢 。主席華許重申抑制通膨目標 ，升
息立場官員數明顯較前次會議增加 ，凸顯聯
準會鷹派政策態度 ，惟利率路徑較市場原先
預期的連續緊縮和緩，市場偏利多解讀。
► 聯準會 本次亦上修 2026-2027年GDP成長預
估，下修失業率預測 ，反映美國 經濟與勞動
市場尚未明顯轉弱；另一方面 ，能源價格上
升與成本轉嫁使整體及核心通膨預估再度上

修；景氣具韌性 、通膨回落速度偏慢 ，使聯
準會仍有條件維持較緊縮的政策立場 。在高
利率環 境可 能延 續 下，股 票評價 仍面 臨壓
力，配置上可相對偏向獲利與現金流穩定 、
防禦性較高的產業；債券則以中短天期 、高
信評企業債為主要方向 ，把握目前較高的收
益率。長天期公債仍受通膨 、財政紀律及期
限溢酬上升影響 ，宜待通膨與景氣出現更明
確降溫訊號後，再提高配置。

► 日本8月CPI年增1.9%，油價上漲帶來的外溢
影響，逐漸反應在消費成本上。7月勞工平均
薪資較去年同期成長 4.7%，創1997年1月以
來最大增幅；薪資增速追上通膨 ，帶動實質
薪資增長 。此外 ，第二季 GDP季增年率達
1.4%優於預期，8月製造業PMI因AI及半導體
需求暢旺上升，7月出口年增率高達23.2%，
創2022年10月來最快增速 。整體通膨升溫加
上景氣復甦，為日銀升息提供底氣。
► 日本央行會後 聲明稿表示：金融環境實質利
率偏低 ，將視情況調整貨幣政策 ，並強調升
息後金融環境仍維持寬鬆 ，未如市場預期的
鷹派，使日股持續上漲 、日圓回貶 。考量日
銀呵護 寬鬆金融環境 ，侷限日圓續強力 道，
但在美 國施 壓與 日 銀未來 仍可能 升息 背景
下，日圓難回到過去極弱 狀態。相較過去 ，
現在升值的日圓對日本出口 、科技股壓力可
控，日本10年公債殖利率來到3%，利率上升
有利 金融 股 ，投 資 短期或 以金融 、內 需為

主，科技、半導體股逢回可分批布局。
經濟與通膨升溫，日銀加快緊縮步調

### Page 7

投資焦點低波價值防禦
► 美、歐、日主要央行連袂升息 ，加上美伊衝
突惡化 ，國際油價高漲 ，通膨疑慮與成熟國
家債務壓力 ，使全球多數國家公債殖利率衝
高，市場擔憂高利率與高油價可能拖累經濟
成長，全球股債匯市承壓 、波動上升 ，並以
此前領漲多頭行情的晶片 、半導體和科技股
最明顯 。觀察今年以來 ，不止股市波動度上
升，債市的波動更不亞於 股票市場 ，統計過
去，當VIX恐慌指數與MOVE債券波動度走高
時，低波動或防禦股的表現 ，往往比成長股
更好，顯示當市場震盪加劇時 ，資金傾向轉
往穩健資產停泊。
► 最近一次聯準會升息 ，為2022 年因疫情爆
發、供應鏈中斷 ，導致通膨大幅上揚 ，迫使
央行採取緊縮貨幣政策 ，和目前通膨上升促
央行升息背景 類似。前次聯準會啟動升息後
數月，成長股 、費城半導體股表現受抑 ，防
禦股、價值股和低波動族群 ，在剛開始升息
的環境下 ，表現相對抗跌 。考量目前聯準 會
籠罩在升息陰霾下，可增加相關類股配置。
對等關稅宣布後，內需防禦股獲利下修幅度最少 最近一次聯準會升息後，防禦低波高息和價值股突出美國股債波動度上升時，低波動、防禦股表現較佳

金融市場波動上升，增配低波、防禦價值股更穩健

### Page 8

8
► 觀察最近一季 ，資訊科技 、通訊服務與非核
心消費等High Beta類股，表現明顯落後於大
盤；反觀價值股 、防禦股和低波動等因子 ，
甚至核 心消 費與 高 股利等 傳統類 防禦 性產
業，報酬相對較 穩健，受資金青睞 。顯示在
經歷過 上半 年科 技 、半導 體股的 大漲 行情
後，市場預期高 利率與地緣政治 風險可能維
持更久時間 ，重新聚焦在獲利持久、現金易
落袋為安的防禦型資產，更能持盈保泰。
► 從預估本 益比看，目前金融僅 16倍、防禦股
17.5倍、價值股 (以IWD ETF代表)17.9倍、
低波動 (以USMV ETF代表)19.6倍，皆低於
標普500指數 現在 的21.3倍，近月資金流向
物美價廉的低波動價值 、防禦族群，評價越
低、漲幅越高 。展望後市，AI長線基本面仍
持續看好 ，股市回檔或為評價 修正而 非盈餘
下滑，市場波動度提升之際 ，建議不過度 集
中於單一 產業或 個股，多元配置適度增加 低
波動 、 防禦 和價 值股，分 批布局 以控管 風

險，料將更能在震盪加劇時安穩求利。

### Page 9

9
股市
 美、歐、日央行升息抑通膨 ，利率創高添股市波動風險 ，加上成熟國家債務攀升 ，科技巨擘亦舉債
投入資本支出 ，未來獲利與變現 能力持續被市場檢視 ，金融市場震盪不減 。美伊衝突升溫，近期可
透過防禦、低波動或價值 股來降低風險；科技 及AI半導體等發展趨勢不變 ，獲利基本面佳 ，長線發
展可期，宜採逢回分批布局方式，分散國家與產業配置。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，全球利率彈升有利銀行股 ，歐、日銀行股基本面佳 ，歐洲國防軍工
仍具投資吸引力。
策略風格：短期可增加防禦股配置，中長
期看好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分
可關注金融等產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲金融、國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升，應避免超長天期債券投資 ，預期年底前美國 10年期公債利率將於 4.5%-5.0%
震盪，可逢利率走升時，以中短天期債券進行鎖利領息 。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流與還款
能力，聚焦風險調整後利差具吸引力者，包含金融、科技、通訊及公用事業等產業。
 可以非美貨幣債券如歐元或澳幣計價投資級債券，分散單一貨幣風險。

債種：中天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金融、
科技、通訊及公用事業等
衛星配置：非美貨幣債券分散風險
匯市
 聯準會升息且態度偏鷹，美元高檔盤整。
 日本銀行9月升息後，年底前或明年初仍可能再度緊縮，日圓震盪加大偏升。
美元高檔整理，日圓震盪加大偏升
商品
 沙烏地阿拉伯關閉輸油管線 ，加上荷姆茲海峽局勢緊張 ，能源供需失衡 ，油價年底前維持 偏高水位。
 短期受美國雙率走高有壓 ，惟美日財政赤字壓力 與去美元化使全球央行買盤依舊 ，提供黃金中長期
穩定支撐。
短期黃金有壓，中長期維持偏多看法

### Page 16

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

Source PDF: all report pdf/Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026EDITORIAL
POSTCARD FROM BANGKOK IMF MEETINGS: DON’T TAKE RESILIENCE FOR GRANTED
1 See EcoNews for more details.
The global economy’s unexpected resilience might have invited the self-congratulation that is a trademark of internatio-
nal official meetings. But policymakers gathered in Bangkok for the 2026 IMF Annual Meetings were wearily aware that
“continued resilience cannot be taken for granted”, as noted in the prologue to the closing Chair’s Statement to the Inter-

national Monetary and Financial Committee meeting. Growth forecasts remain in line with those made a year ago, when
interest rates were much lower and no energy shock was in the cards, and even with those made in April, when a short
shock was the base case. But what has sustained global growth against the odds so far could falter before the headwinds
ease: oil buffers are being depleted; fiscal policy urgently needs to shift from support to deficit reduction as interest rates

remain persistently higher; and the AI boom that has been a salvatory growth engine comes with serious questions about
its sustainability and long-term economic effects. In addition to these global issues, the combination of fiscal challenges
and elections in France and the US were seen as key risks, while some topics that had until recently been in the spotlight
found themselves overshadowed.
BETTER GROWTH THAN EXPECTED FOR MOST , WORSE INFLATION FOR ALL

Despite the energy shock and surge in interest rates, the IMF's
latest global growth forecast is unchanged from a year ago and April
— at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 — although the geographical
breakdown has shifted: war-exposed countries and many energy im -
porters have been downgraded, while AI-exposed countries have been
upgraded. Asia is the standout winner, expected to account for 68% of
global growth in 2026 — a feat for a region highly dependent on Gulf

energy. Compared to July, the growth forecast for advanced economies
is revised up to 2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany) and remains
at 1.8% in 2027. Among emerging markets, the IMF upgraded Asia and
downgraded other regions. Meanwhile, global headline inflation is ex-
pected to rise to 4.7% in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before
easing to 4.1% in 2027 (revised +0.2pp)1.
AI OPTIMISM DOMINATES, BUT WITH SOME HEAVY UNANSWERED
QUESTIONS

The topic of AI dominated the Meetings. The broad consensus was that
it is transformational and offers great opportunities to all countries.
But, for now, it is producing a mixed bag of economic effects: a power-
ful boost to global demand, directly through an investment and trade
boom and indirectly through wealth effects from stock-market gains;
but also much higher interest rates (fuelled by the borrowing needed
to fund the AI build-out, and higher prices for chips, all the products

they go into, and the electricity and water data centres need).
Beyond this shared diagnosis, strong arguments could be heard on both
sides of several critical questions. The most pressing being: will AI ge-
nerate earnings commensurate with current expectations? If not — or
even if expectations are merely downgraded — expect large declines in
AI-related asset prices, a sudden stop in funding and a significant blow
to growth wherever AI has been a key engine. This is not in anyone’s

baseline for next year, but it is clearly a top risk. To what extent will AI
boost productivity over five or ten years? The IMF incorporates almost
no boost in its five-year baseline, but presents an upside scenario in
which it does. Will AI create more jobs than it destroys, raise or reduce
labour’s share of income, prove inflationary or deflationary beyond the
near term, and strengthen cyber resilience or turbocharge attacks?
And how should it be taxed to preserve the fiscal base and offset unde-

sirable labour-market effects?
A MIXED POLICY SCORECARD
Monetary policy has appropriately taken a hawkish turn nearly eve -
rywhere, and an increase in rate rises is likely in key economies, though
not enough to threaten growth. Eurozone central bankers were parti -
cularly clear in sticking to their “moderate response” playbook.
Fiscal policy, however, has yet to adjust to the end of cheap debt. With
public debt high in most countries, a growing share of national bud -

gets will be absorbed by interest payments. Yet very few countries are
consolidating. The IMF’s top call to action was to address this urgent-
ly, especially among the worst offenders — the US and France. Fiscal
restraint would also help central banks curb inflation, reducing the
need for higher interest rates.
Policies to redress global imbalances are not closer to being adop -
ted than at previous IMF Meetings, but they were not particular -
ly highlighted by the IMF this time, beyond the mantra that “the US

needs to save more, China to consume more, and Europe to invest
more”. There were reportedly good discussions behind closed doors
— at the G7 on “non-market policies and practices (NMPPs)” — and
encouraging early steps in a long journey in Europe-China talks. That
is better than nothing, but hardly enough to expect progress within a
reasonable timeframe. Meanwhile, the risks of a disorderly unwind will
keep rising.
TWO ZONES OF CONCERN IN FOCUS: FRANCE AND THE US

France was a top concern for many participants. They feared a repeat
of the 2010–2012 Eurozone debt crisis, seeing a country with public
debt approaching 120% of GDP , sub-par growth, a poor record of fiscal
consolidation, riots in the streets and presidential elections six mon -
ths away, with populist candidates leading the polls. French and Eu -
ropean policymakers, alongside private-sector participants, presented
a different picture, namely: a fiscal situation that is serious, but is

being managed (the draft budget provides for meaningful adjustment
and is very likely to be adopted without being weakened); a widening
consensus from centre-left to far-right which favours a return to fiscal
responsibility, in line with a similar shift in public opinion; and the
electoral process is only beginning, with the outcome still wide open —
not least because the candidates’ names and numbers are not known.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
It was also pointed out that the quantum of fiscal adjustment required
is not out of line with previous consolidation episodes in France in the
2010s and the 1990s. Still, most participants left not much reassured
and expecting a bumpy ride ahead.
US participants outside government saw a growing likelihood of a De -
mocratic sweep in the midterm elections, Either way, they expected

little fiscal consolidation, despite a manifestly challenging fiscal path
that may end up causing volatility in financial markets. Non-US parti -
cipants also feared growing policy unpredictability.
OVERSHADOWED TOPICS: THE STARS OF RECENT IMF MEETINGS
Tariffs were entirely absent from the agenda and conversations. Toke-
nised finance (rather than the narrower topic of stablecoins) still fea -
tured, but far less prominently: as the field takes shape, discussion

has shifted from the conceptual what and why to the technical how.
Climate change and low-income countries remained on the agenda,
but far less in focus than at previous Meetings. The sheer number of
issues demanding attention may be overwhelming financial policyma-
kers’ bandwidth.
*****
All in all, while the global context remains extraordinarily challenging,
it’s comforting to see that global financial policy makers are broadly
alert to both risks and opportunities, and to what needs to be done for

the latter to prevail. Now they need to deliver.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

Document 357: Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#china #asia #aud
Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain
By Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics, Westpac
16:03 October 19 2026 Share
Stimulus is required if success in 2026 is to be sustained through 2027 and 2028.
China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3 2026, quarterly growth accelerating from 0.9% in Q2 to 1.2%
in Q3. Annual growth lifted from 4.3% to 4.5% as a result; though, on a year-to-date basis, growth
edged down 0.1ppts to 4.6%. The Q3 result keeps authorities on track to achieve their 4.5-5.0%ytd

ambition for 2026, and also provides a little more time to accelerate domestic demand into 2027.

### Page 2

Broad-based stimulus is required because net exports’ contribution to growth will likely abate in coming
quarters now that the trade surplus is back at record highs, and as many Chinese firms are
increasingly splitting their focus between expanding domestic capacity and offshore production
networks.

While annual retail sales growth accelerated from 0.4% to 1.1% in September, the Q3 year-to-date
growth pulse of 1.1% is just a quarter of the gain reported a year ago. Automobiles, construction
materials and jewellery have been particular heavy detractors over the past year, but most of the other
sub-categories also lack momentum. As we detailed in our September Market Outlook, this is not
because households are cash constrained, but rather as sentiment is extremely weak and the outlook
for employment and wealth highly uncertain. Key for wealth is that house prices for new and
established homes continue to decline month-on-month (respectively 0.1% and 0.3% in September)
five years into the downturn. Property investment’s ongoing decline (-20.6%ytd) suggests this
persistent weakness in sales and prices is demand driven.

While total fixed asset investment has been an outright negative for annual GDP growth since April, a
recovery is not beyond either the public or private sector’s capacity. Rather, across the economy,
entities are waiting for direction and incentive from the Central Government. This is likely to come by
early-2027 to offset the loss of support from trade, and as downside risks to global growth become
more apparent. Helpfully, industrial production continues to grow at a robust pace, 5.3%ytd, borrowing
rates for businesses and households are low, and authorities are beginning to provide banks with
additional lending capacity. For larger businesses, anecdotes suggest officials are also encouraging
major institutional investors to increase their structural allocation to domestic equities, building lasting 
appetite for new equity issuance, which in turn will fund future waves of real economic capacity and
income generation.

We remain comfortable with the view that Chinese GDP growth will hold at or slightly above 4.5%
through 2026–2028. But, the longer the Central Government waits to trigger new real investment
across both the private and public sectors, the higher the likelihood of poor sentiment becoming
entrenched and downside risks crystallising. If authorities wait until after net exports’ contribution has
faded, even holding annual aggregate growth above 4.0% could become a significant challenge. For
this reason, we expect material stimulus initiatives to be announced in the next 3 to 6 months to reset
the property sector and turn the business investment trend.

Introduction 
With trillions of dollars, euros and yen changing hands every day, foreign 
exchange is indisputably the largest and most liquid financial market in the 
world. Yet despite its size, the currency market is also one of the least 
understood. Unlike bonds or equities, there is no widely accepted framework for 
explaining currency moves, which reflect a complex interplay of 
macroeconomics, capital flows, market structure, investor behaviour and 
(geo)politics. 
Over the past two decades, advances in technology and market infrastructure 
have transformed how currencies are traded, placing FX at the forefront of 
broader changes in global finance. Yet because currencies sit at the intersection 
of national economies, the FX market is also fundamentally connected to 
international trade and cross-border capital flows. It is therefore increasingly 
exposed to the forces reshaping the global economic landscape—from 
geopolitical fragmentation and changing trade relationships to shifts in 
regulation, payments infrastructure and the architecture of the international 
monetary system. 
This Deutsche Bank Currency Book brings together academic, industry and 
Deutsche Bank expertise to explain how FX markets function, how exchange 
rates are determined, how investors seek to generate returns from currencies, 
and how FX risk can be managed by investors and corporates alike. Our aim 
throughout the Book is to set out the enduring principles driving markets 
alongside the developments defining FX today. Ultimately, the structure of the FX 
market is not simply a question of how currencies trade, but of who controls the 
financial infrastructure through which money moves across borders. The 
evolution of FX is increasingly intertwined with questions of monetary 
sovereignty, financial resilience and geopolitical power. 
Chapter 1 reviews the structure of the FX market, examining its participants, 
ecosystem and evolution over time. Chapter 2 discusses exchange rate 
determination, moving from traditional academic theories to modern macro-
financial approaches and practical decision frameworks. Chapter 3 reviews FX 
as an asset class and the evidence on currency investing, while Chapter 4 
considers currency hedging for both investors and corporates. Finally, Chapter 5 
discusses the future of FX, including the impact of technology, payment systems 
and digital assets.

Introduction
With trillions of dollars, euros and yen changing hands every day, foreign exchange
is indisputably the largest and most liquid financial market in the world. Yet despite
its size, the currency market is also one of the least understood. Unlike bonds or
equities, there is no widely accepted framework for explaining currency moves,
which reflect a complex  interplay of macroeconomics,  capital flows,  market
structure, investor behaviour and (geo)politics.

Over the past two decades, advances in technology and market infrastructure have
transformed how currencies are traded, placing FX at the  forefront of broader
changes in global finance. Yet because currencies sit at the intersection of national
economies, the FX market is also fundamentally connected to international trade
and cross-border capital flows. It is therefore increasingly exposed to the forces
reshaping the global economic landscape—from geopolitical fragmentation and

changing trade relationships to shifts in regulation, payments infrastructure and
the architecture of the international monetary system.
This Deutsche Bank Currency Book brings together  academic, industry and
Deutsche Bank expertise to explain how FX markets function, how exchange rates
are determined, how investors seek to generate returns from currencies, and how
FX risk can be managed by investors and corporates alike. Our aim throughout
the Book is to set out the enduring principles driving markets alongside

the developments defining FX today. Ultimately, the structure of the FX
market is not simply a question of how currencies trade, but of who
controls the financial infrastructure through which money moves across
borders. The evolution of FX is increasingly intertwined with questions
of monetary sovereignty, financial resilience and geopolitical power.

Chapter 1 reviews the structure of the FX market, examining its participants,

ecosystem and evolution over time. Chapter 2 discusses exchange rate

### Page 7

The Currency Book
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
Introduction
The foreign exchange market is the world’s largest and most liquid financial market,
but it differs fundamentally from a centralised exchange. Trading takes place across
a fragmented, largely over-the-counter network of banks, non-bank liquidity
providers, electronic venues and end-users, using instruments ranging from spot
and forwards to swaps, futures and options. How do transactions move through

this network? Who provides liquidity, who consumes it and what risks arise
between execution and settlement? This chapter maps the FX ecosystem from
instruments and trading infrastructure to the participants ultimately responsible for
currency demand.
1.1 Instruments
The FX market is not one single product. Instead, it is made up of a group of
instruments that allow market participants to exchange currencies today, lock in an
exchange rate for the future and/or hedge against adverse exchange-rate moves.

Below, we outline the main instruments in FX markets and their users.
Spot: This is the simplest and most widely known FX instrument. When market
participants refer to “the exchange-rate” or the “spot exchange-rate,” they
generally mean the price agreed today for exchanging one currency for another on
the standard spot settlement date. For most major currency pairs spot settlement
is two business days later (T+2).1  For example, a spot purchase of EUR 1 million

against the USD at a EUR/USD exchange-rate of 1.20 means that the buyer receives
EUR 1 million in his or her bank account on the settlement date and the seller
receives USD 1.2 million from the buyer on the same date.
1 There are exceptions, for example USD/CAD settles T+1.

### Page 8

The Currency Book
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
By convention, the first currency in an exchange-rate quotation is known as the
base currency, while the second is the quote currency. The exchange-rate therefore
represents the number of units of the quote currency required to purchase one unit
of the base currency. In USD/JPY, for example, the USD is the base currency and the
exchange-rate indicates the number of Japanese yen required to purchase one US

dollar. A USD/JPY exchange-rate of 160 means 1 USD can be exchanged for 160
JPY.
Spot transactions are the most commonly used foreign exchange payments for
cross-border payments for goods and services, the purchase of foreign financial
assets, as well as portfolio rebalancing and proprietary trading. In 2025, the Bank
for International Settlements (BIS) estimates spot turnover at $3trn per day,
accounting for 31% of global over-the-counter (OTC) FX turnover.

Forwards and futures: An (outright) forward is similar to a spot transaction, but is
instead an agreement to exchange two currencies at a rate (price) agreed today, but
with settlement taking place at a date more than T+2 days in the future. They are
also traded OTC. Forwards are most commonly used by those who have a known
future cash flow and want to remove uncertainty around the future exchange rate.
In 2025, the BIS estimates that they account for 19% of global turnover, or $1.8trn

per day.
Futures are economically similar to forwards but are standardised contracts traded
on exchanges and centrally cleared. This provides transparent pricing and reduces
bilateral counterparty risk, but offers less flexibility over contract size, maturity and
settlement terms than an OTC forward. Exchange-traded FX futures remain
considerably smaller than the OTC FX market.
Non-deliverable forwards (NDFs): An NDF is a cash-settled forward commonly

used for currencies where convertibility or offshore deliverability is restricted. Two
parties agree today on an exchange rate for a future date, but unlike a standard
forward, the underlying currencies are not exchanged. At maturity, the agreed NDF
rate is compared with a pre-specified fixing or reference rate. The difference is
usually settled in USD. NDFs are commonly used by corporates, banks and
investors to hedge or take exposure to currencies subject to capital controls.

Swaps: An FX swap combines two transactions: an exchange of currencies on an
initial date and a reverse exchange at a later date, with both exchange rates agreed
when the swap is initiated. They are used by institutions that hold cash in one
currency (e.g. EUR) but need funding in another (e.g. USD) for investment, hedging
or other needs. They are by far the largest FX instrument by turnover, with the BIS
estimating turnover of $4trn per day in April 2025, 42% of global FX turnover. A

swap is, in effect, a form of collateralised borrowing, with the collateral being cash
in a different currency to that of the currency borrowed.
Cross-currency (XCCY) swaps: A cross-currency swap is similar to an FX swap in
that principal amounts denominated in two different currencies are typically
exchanged at inception and reversed at maturity. The key difference is that a cross-
currency swap also involves periodic exchanges of interest payments denominated

in the two currencies over the life of the contract. These payments may be fixed or
floating. Cross-currency swaps therefore allow market participants to transform
both the currency denomination and, where relevant, the interest-rate
characteristics of an asset or liability.

### Page 9

The Currency Book
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
For example:
n Suppose a European corporate issues a USD-denominated bond to US-
based investors. It receives the bond proceeds in USD, but must
periodically pay interest in USD and repay the USD principal when the bond
matures. However, because the corporate primarily earns revenue in EUR,
it needs to convert these USD liabilities into EUR liabilities to avoid currency
and potentially interest-rate risk.

n The corporate removes FX and interest-rate risk by entering into a xccy
swap, where, in essence, it converts this USD-liability into a EUR-liability.2
n They are commonly used by banks, corporates and investors that need to
raise money in one currency but need funds in another. The basis is the extra
spread added to one side of the swap to balance unequal demand for
funding in the two currencies. See Section 2.11 for more on cross-currency
basis and here.

Options: An option gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell
one currency against another at a specified exchange rate during a specified period.
Options are used to hedge currency risk or speculate on currency movements and
are particularly useful when the end-user wants an asymmetric payoff profile.
Unlike a forward, an option can limit losses from an adverse currency movement
while allowing the holder to retain some or all of the benefit from a favourable

movement. In 2025, they represented around 7% of global FX turnover. Options are
widely used by corporates and investors for hedging, and by investors and dealers
to express directional or volatility views.
The simplest and most widely used instruments are vanilla options, which give the
holder the right to buy (a call) or sell (a put) a currency at a predetermined strike
price. A European-style option can be exercised only at expiry, while an American-

style option can be exercised at any point up to expiry. More structured products
include barrier options, whose payoff is activated or extinguished if the exchange
rate reaches a specified level—for example, knock-in and knock-out options. One-
touch and no-touch options pay a fixed amount depending on whether the
exchange-rate does, or does not, reach a predetermined level during the life of the
option. These instruments are commonly used to express views not just on the

direction of a currency pair, but also on the probability, timing and magnitude of
exchange-rate moves.
1.2 FX spot trading life cycle and risks
Having discussed the main FX instruments, the next step is to examine how these
transactions are executed and settled in practice. The complete trade life cycle,
meaning the sequence of events and processes involved in executing a trade, is
crucial to understanding how FX transactions work. We therefore describe below

the broad steps involved in trading FX, focusing on spot transactions. They are:
1.  Pre-trade: This stage encompasses all the necessary preparations before
a trade is executed, including client onboarding, platform selection, credit
checks, and regulatory compliance.
2 Specifically, it exchanges the USD bond proceeds for EUR at inception, receives USD interest payments
(from the swap counterparty to pay U.S. bond investors) and pays EUR interest (to the swap

counterparty) over the life of the swap, and receives USD principal (from the swap counterparty to pay
U.S. investors) in exchange for EUR principal at maturity. The USD receipts from the swap are used to
meet the bond’s USD interest and principal payments, effectively converting the USD-denominated
bond into a EUR-denominated liability.

### Page 10

The Currency Book
Page 10 Deutsche Bank AG
2.  Trade execution: This is the point where the trade is agreed upon and
executed at a specific exchange rate, creating a legally binding contract.
3.  Trade clearing: This stage involves validating trade details and managing
counterparty credit risk before settlement, either bilaterally or through a
central clearing counterparty (CCP). In centrally cleared FX markets, the
CCP becomes the buyer to every seller and the seller to every buyer,

reducing direct counterparty exposures between market participants.
Specialist FX CCPs, such as LCH's ForexClear, clear both non-deliverable
and deliverable FX products. While FX spot transactions can be cleared,
they are not subject to mandatory clearing requirements. ForexClear
supports 25 non-deliverable forward (NDF) currency pairs, 9 non-
deliverable option (NDO) currency pairs, and 8 deliverable (Spot, Forwards
and Options) currency pairs, at the time of writing.

4.  Trade settlement: This final stage represents the completion of the
transaction, with the actual exchange of currencies taking place.
Settlement for spot trades typically occurs two business days (T+2) after
the trade date, although exceptions exist. For example, USD/CAD settles
on a T+1 basis.
Figure 2: FX Spot Trade Life Cycle
Source : Deutsche Bank
Risks surrounding FX spot trading
The over-the-counter (OTC) nature of the FX market, while offering flexibility and

adaptability to participant needs, does present certain risks that are often more
pronounced compared to exchange-traded markets. Below we discuss some of the
key risks in FX.
Credit risk: Credit risk in FX trading, stemming from a counterparty's potential
inability to fulfill its obligations, is present throughout the trade lifecycle:
n Pre-trade: Counterparty creditworthiness is a critical consideration in the
A Central Clearing Counterparty (CCP), also

known as a Central Counterparty, stands
between buyers and sellers in financial
markets, becoming the buyer to every
seller and the seller to every buyer. This
mitigates counterparty risk.

### Page 11

The Currency Book
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
pre-trade stage of FX transactions. A thorough assessment should involve
evaluating the potential counterparty's financial strength, credit rating, and
reputation. Choosing a creditworthy counterparty significantly reduces the
risk of default throughout the trade lifecycle.
n Post-trade (Clearing and Settlement): While credit risk exists in both the
clearing (post-execution/pre-settlement) and settlement stages of a spot

FX trade, it primarily materializes during settlement. This stage, involving
the actual exchange of currencies, carries the highest risk due to the
potential for settlement failure. This risk, often referred to as settlement risk,
arises when one party delivers their currency while the counterparty
defaults. Cross-border trades are particularly vulnerable due to time zone
differences, which can create a window where one party has performed but
the other has not. Additionally, mark-to-market risk from FX rate

fluctuations between trade and settlement dates can exacerbate
settlement risk by increasing potential losses from a default. This is
particularly pronounced with forward contracts, where the longer pre-
settlement period leads to greater mark-to-market risk and, consequently,
higher settlement risk.
Market Risk: This risk in FX trading arises from potential losses due to adverse
exchange-rate movements. This is influenced by factors like economic data,

geopolitical events, and interest rates. For customers, asymmetric information,
where one party has more or better information than another, can lead to inefficient
pricing and increased volatility, thereby exacerbating market risk.
n For dealers, a key component of market risk is inventory management risk
– the potential for losses from unfavourable price movements in the
currencies they hold to facilitate client trading. Effective inventory
management, including position limits and hedging, mitigates this risk.

Liquidity Risk: This refers to the overall market's ability to facilitate the buying and
selling of a currency pair quickly and efficiently at a desired price. It's influenced by
factors such as market depth, trading volume, volatility, and fragmentation.
Operational Risk: This encompasses risks associated with trade processing,
settlement, and technology. Errors in trade execution, system failures, and fraud
can all lead to significant losses.
Regulatory Risk: This refers to the risk arising from changes in regulations or their

interpretation. The evolving regulatory landscape in FX, particularly concerning
non-bank dealers and systemic risk, can impact market participants and create
uncertainty.
1.3 Market participants
The FX market comprises a variety of participants with different objectives. FX
market participants fall into two broad categories: financial and non-financial.
Financial participants can act as both liquidity providers and consumers, while non-
financial participants act largely as liquidity consumers.

Financial participants: In the FX market, financial participants are primarily banks
acting as dealers.
n Dealers: These are mainly large commercial banks, investment banks and
securities houses that participate in the inter-dealer market for proprietary

### Page 12

The Currency Book
Page 12 Deutsche Bank AG
trading. 3 and/or for meeting customer demand through market-making.4
n Institutional investors: These are mutual funds, pension funds, insurance
companies and endowment funds, collectively referred to as “real-money
investors”, which need to buy currencies to satisfy end-user demand for an
underlying asset. For instance, a UK pension fund needs to buy USD to
purchase US equities. These investors also engage in hedging, proprietary

trading and risk management activities.
n Hedge Funds & Principal Trading Firms (PTFs): Hedge funds are
investment funds that follow a broad range of investment strategies and are
generally not subject to the same borrowing and leverage restrictions as
traditional investment funds. They are users of FX and derivatives for
speculative purposes. PTFs are principal trading firms, such as XTX
Markets and Citadel Securities, that hedge or speculate for their own

account and also participate in market-making. This category may include
high-frequency trading firms that employ high-speed algorithmic trading
strategies.
Non financial institutions: Non-financial participants, such as corporations
involved in international trade, retail and the official sector, also primarily consume
liquidity.
n Corporates are active in currency markets primarily to manage FX
exposures 5  arising from international trade, foreign-currency financing,

overseas operations and cross-border mergers and acquisitions (M&A).6
n Official sector: This category includes central banks, sovereign wealth
funds, international financial institutions, such as the IMF and BIS,
development banks and government agencies. Monetary-policy, financial-
stability and mercantilist considerations have all, at various times,
motivated central-bank intervention in FX.7
n Other: This category includes all other market participants, including retail

participants. Retail participants engage in FX both to speculate and to meet
transactional demand arising from travel, trade or investment.
1.4 Profit versus non-profit seekers
In 2025, daily FX turnover reached $9.5 trillion according to the BIS triennial survey
(BIS, 2025) making currency trading the world's largest financial market. Yet,
size and liquidity do not necessarily imply efficiency. Unlike most equity or
bond transactions, a large share of FX activity serves an underlying

transactional purpose. For instance, corporates must convert revenues and
meet foreign- currency invoices; institutional investors hedge overseas
assets; and dealers intermediate these flows. For these participants,
expected FX returns may be secondary, or even irrelevant, to the decision to
trade. In this section, we investigate
3 Proprietary trading is a profit-seeking activity that dealers once engaged in more extensively, but which
has been severely constrained by regulatory change. Most notably, the Volcker Rule, implemented in

2014-15 under the Dodd-Frank regulations, bans banks from engaging in proprietary trading activities,
including many foreign exchange instruments.
4 Market making: This is an activity that seeks to capture returns by matching buyers and sellers and
charging a price for the service through the bid-offer spread. This spread represents the price that
liquidity seekers must pay to access the market and is a “frictional” cost of executing transactions. As

such, market-making is a liquidity-provision service rather than a source of the ‘excess returns'
associated with activities such as proprietary trading

(2) Mandate constraints relate to limits

constraints on investment allocations and

Approach 1: The Triennial BIS Survey of FX market volumes
We use the 2025 BIS Triennial Survey to update Deutsche Bank's previous work

(Saravelos et al, 2018) and estimate the share of FX turnover initiated with a profit-
seeking currency objective. We construct a "realistic scenario", which applies what
we deem to be "realistic" assumptions about participants’ ability and mandate to
take FX risk.8 We find that profit-seeking activity accounts for approximately 40%
of turnover under the realistic scenario.9
Financial participants
n Dealers: Dealer-to-dealer activity accounts for approximately 47% of total

daily FX turnover ($4.44trn) and we classify 10% of dealer flow as profit-
seeking ($444bn/day). Given that much of the daily FX turnover reflects
market-making, we assume that inter-dealer transactions conducted by
voice are not profit-seeking and that only electronic transactions
conducted through order-matching systems such as EBS are profit-
seeking.
n Hedge Funds and PTFs: Hedge Funds and PTFs account for approximately
$760bn (8%) of headline daily FX turnover and we classify 90% of this as

profit-seeking ($684bn/day). The $684bn/day figure represents the profit-
seeking nature of hedge-funds where the remaining 10% reflects the
ensure that the mark-to-market value of their hedge does not introduce unwanted volatility in the
income statement. IFRS 9 provided an updated set of standards for hedge accounting, designed to
increase flexibility and better link financial reporting to firms’ risk management practices. However,
hedge accounting still places limitations on the types of instruments and kinds of hedges that can be

implemented. For example, to qualify for hedge accounting, corporates must adhere to hedge
effectiveness and demonstrate that an economic relationship between the underlying cash flow or
asset and the hedge exists, as well as quantitative limits to hedge ratios.
7 Exchange rates are explicitly targeted by some central banks as part of their overall monetary policy
framework (for instance the Monetary Authority of Singapore or the Hong Kong Monetary Authority);

other central banks have intervened to defend their inflation target or financial stability reasons (most
recently, the Swiss National Bank); and others to prevent competitiveness losses in global trade.

expansion of PTFs to act not only as profit-seekers but also as liquidity
providers.

Approach 2: Commitment of Traders CFTC data
While the BIS data provide the most comprehensive view of the global FX market,
the survey is only updated every three years. For a higher-frequency perspective, we
therefore complement it with the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission’s
Commitments of Traders (CoT) report, which is published weekly and provides a

breakdown of positions in exchange-traded currency futures and options,
principally those traded on the Chicago Mercantile Exchange (CME). The report
sho
ws the number of outstanding long and short contracts held by different
categories of market participant, based on positions as of each Tuesday. Although
the futures market represents only a small subset of total global FX activity—most
of which takes place over the counter—the CoT data provide a timely and consistent

measure of how speculative and hedging positions are evolving.
11 Corporates use FX to settle foreign-currency revenues and expenses, finance cross-border activity,
hedge forecast cash flows and manage earnings volatility. Governments similarly transact to meet an
underlying funding or expenditure requirement.
12 Some investors trade currencies to earn returns, while other transactions relate to travel, remittances
or purchases of foreign assets

### Page 16

The Currency Book
Page 16 Deutsche Bank AG
In the traditional or “legacy” CoT report, reportable positions  are divided into
commercial and non-commercial traders, alongside a residual category of smaller,
non-reportable positions. Commercial traders are generally entities that use futures
primarily in connection  with an underlying business activity  and are therefore
commonly interpreted as hedgers—for  example, corporates or financial
institutions managing currency exposures. We classify these positions as liquidity

seeking. Non-commercial traders,  by contrast, are participants  whose futures
activity is not classified as commercial hedging and typically includes hedge funds,
commodity trading advisers and other leveraged investors. We therefore treat
non-commercial positions as profit-seeking. Figure 7 and Figure 8 show how the
relative size of the profit-seeking FX market has developed over time. Using one-
year rolling averages to reduce the noise of the data, we see that roughly 30-40%

of the futures market (EUR/USD, GBP/USD, USD/JPY) is profit-seeking.

Approach 3: Options market DTCC data
Following the implementation of the Dodd-Frank Act, OTC FX options falling within
the US regulatory perimeter are required to be reported to a CFTC-registered swap
data repository (SDR). This includes transactions involving US market participants,
among others. DTCC operates one of the largest data repositories and therefore

captures a substantial share of global institutional FX option activity. We estimate
that the DTCC data capture more than  20% of global FX derivative  volumes,
providing another way of estimating the relative importance of profit- and non-
profit-seeking activity in the FX market.
While the data does not provide  client granularity, we can back out the  likely
motivation of the transaction by distinguishing between out-of-the-money and in-
the-money option trades. We argue that strikes preferred by the different client

groups will likely differ, with hedgers favouring out of the money options (0-25
delta). While speculators focus on directional leverage, the latter often translates
to options with strikes nearer to the money (25-75 delta).
Figure 9 shows the split in options volumes for 2025/26 across the two investor
categories. We find that option volumes are roughly split equally between investors
and hedgers. FX options are instruments that are favoured by hedge funds over

corporates and institutional investors, so the results here likely materially overstate
the relative importance of profit-maximizing participants in other markets. Again,
this shows the FX market is split roughly 50/50 by those who are profit-seeking and
those who are not.
